\displaystyle\,-2\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(\tilde{f}(X)-f(X))^{2}\big]+2\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(\tilde{f}(X)-f(X))(\tilde{f}(X)-f_{\diamond}(X))\big]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,-8\delta^{2}K^{2}-2\sqrt{4\delta^{2}K^{2}}\sqrt{4\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-8\delta^{2}K^{2}-8\delta K\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})},

(B.22)

where, in the third equality, we have added and subtracted the
term

2\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(\tilde{f}(X)-f(X))\tilde{f}(X)\big]
.
Now let

\mathcal{S}:=\{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}:\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]\leq\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-\tilde{f}(X))^{2}\big]\}

be the set of all functions satisfying
(
B.21
). Due to
(
B.18
), (
B.19
),
(
B.20
) and
(
B.22
) we have the
following lower bound of interest

\displaystyle\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{S}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{S}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\big\{\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]+L_{1}^{i}(\tilde{f})+L_{2}^{i}(\tilde{f},f_{\diamond})+L_{3}^{i}(\tilde{f})\big\}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{S}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]-8\delta^{2}K^{2}-8\delta K\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}-4\delta K\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]-8\delta^{2}K^{2}-12\delta K\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}.

(B.23)

Next, we construct the aforementioned upper bound of interest. To that end, note that

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\quad=\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]+L_{1}^{i}(\tilde{f})+L_{2}^{i}(\tilde{f},f^{*})+L_{3}^{i}(\tilde{f})\right\},

(B.24)

by (
B.18
). We have already
established upper bounds for
L_{1}^{i}(\tilde{f})
and

L_{3}^{i}(\tilde{f})
in (
B.19
) and
(
B.20
), respectively. In order to control

L_{2}^{i}(\tilde{f},f^{*})
we introduce an auxiliary function. Let

\bar{f}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}
satisfy

\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-\bar{f}^{*}(X))^{2})\big]\leq\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f(X))^{2})\big],

(B.25)

and

\displaystyle\bigg|\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-\bar{f}^{*}(X))^{2}\big]-\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]\bigg|\leq\varepsilon.

(B.26)

Choosing such a
\bar{f}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}
is always possible. If
f

is an
\varepsilon
-minimax solution, i.e., it satisfies
(
B.26
), then choose
\bar{f}^{*}=f
. Otherwise, if
f
is not a
\varepsilon
-minimax solution,
then choose any
\bar{f}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}
that is an
\varepsilon
-minimax
solution (which is always possible). In this case we have that

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-\bar{f}^{*}(X))^{2}\big]-\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]\leq\varepsilon,

and

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f(X))^{2}\big]-\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]\geq\varepsilon,

which implies that (
B.25
) is
satisfied. We can now construct an upper bound on

L_{2}^{i}(\tilde{f},f^{*})
in terms of
L_{2}^{i}(\tilde{f},\bar{f}^{*})

by noting that for all
i\in\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle\big\lvert L_{2}^{i}(\tilde{f},f^{*})\big\rvert=

\displaystyle\,2\big|\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(\tilde{f}(X)-f(X))(f(X)-f^{*}(X))\big]\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,2\big|\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(\tilde{f}(X)-f(X))(f(X)-\bar{f}^{*}(X))\big]\big|

\displaystyle\,+2\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big|(\tilde{f}(X)-f(X))(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))\big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\big\lvert L_{2}^{i}(\tilde{f},\bar{f}^{*})\big\rvert+2\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big|(\tilde{f}(X)-f(X))(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\left[(\tilde{f}(X)-f(X))^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\left[(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))^{2}\right]}+\big\lvert L_{2}^{i}(\tilde{f},\bar{f}^{*})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\big\lvert L_{2}^{i}(\tilde{f},\bar{f}^{*})\big\rvert+4\delta K\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\left[(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))^{2}\right]},

(B.27)

where we used the triangle inequality, Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (
B.17
). Furthermore,
(
B.17
) and (
B.25
) together with Proposition
3.3
yield the following bound

\displaystyle|L_{2}^{i}(\tilde{f},\bar{f}^{*})|=

\displaystyle\,2\big|\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(\tilde{f}(X)-f(X))(f(X)-\bar{f}^{*}(X))\big]\big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(\tilde{f}(X)-f(X))^{2}\big]\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(f(X)-\bar{f}^{*}(X))^{2}\big]}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,2\sqrt{4\delta^{2}K^{2}}\sqrt{4\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,8\delta K\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})},

(B.28)

for any
i\in\mathcal{I}
. Thus, it suffices to construct an upper
bound on the second term in the final expression in
(
B.27
). Direct computation leads
to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]=

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-\bar{f}^{*}(X))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\,+\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\,+2\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-\bar{f}^{*}(X))(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))\big].

Rearranging the terms and applying the triangle inequality and Cauchy-Schwarz results in

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}

\displaystyle\,\big[(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]-\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-\bar{f}^{*}(X))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\,-2\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-\bar{f}^{*}(X))(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\big|\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]-\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]\big|

\displaystyle\,+\big|\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]-\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-\bar{f}^{*}(X))^{2}\big]\big|

\displaystyle\,+2\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big|(Y-\bar{f}^{*}(X))(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,2\varepsilon+2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-\bar{f}^{*}(X))^{2}\big]}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,2\varepsilon+2\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]},

for any
i\in\mathcal{I}
. Here, we used that both
f^{*}
and
\bar{f}^{*}
are
\varepsilon
-minimax solutions with respect to
M
and that
\bar{f}^{*}
satisfies (
B.25
) which implies that

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-\bar{f}^{*}(X))^{2})\big]\leq\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f(X))^{2})\big]=\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}\big]=\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y}),

for any
i\in\mathcal{I}
, as
\xi_{Y}
is unaffected by an intervention on
X
.
Thus,
\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]
must satisfy
\ell(\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big])\leq 0
, where
\ell:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is given by
\ell(z)=z-2\varepsilon-2\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}\sqrt{z}
.
The linear term of
\ell
grows faster than the square
root term, so the largest allowed value of
\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]
coincides with the largest root of
\ell(z)
. The largest root is given by

\displaystyle C^{2}:=2\varepsilon+2\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})+2\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})^{2}+2\varepsilon\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})},

where
(\cdot)^{2}
refers to the square of
C
. Hence, for any

i\in\mathcal{I}
it holds that

\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(\bar{f}^{*}(X)-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]\leq C^{2}.

(B.29)

Hence by (
B.27
), (
B.28
) and (
B.29
) we have that the following upper bound is valid for any
i\in\mathcal{I}
.

\displaystyle\big\lvert L_{2}^{i}(\tilde{f},f^{*})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 8\delta K\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}+4\delta KC.

(B.30)

Thus, using (
B.24
) with
(
B.19
), (
B.20
) and
(
B.30
), we get the following upper bound

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]+4\delta^{2}K^{2}+4\delta KC+12\delta K\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}.

(B.31)

Finally, by combining the bounds (
B.23
)
and (
B.31
) together with
(
B.16
) we get that

\displaystyle\bigg|\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]-\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f^{*}(X))^{2}\big]-\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{M(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\,+4\delta^{2}K^{2}+4\delta KC+12\delta K\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}

\displaystyle\,+8\delta^{2}K^{2}+12\delta K\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon+12\delta^{2}K^{2}+24\delta K\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}+4\delta KC.

(B.32)

Using that all terms are positive, we get that

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}+\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})+2\varepsilon}\leq 2\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}

Hence, (
B.32
) is bounded above by

\displaystyle\varepsilon+12\delta^{2}K^{2}+32\delta K\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}_{M}(\xi_{Y})}+4\sqrt{2}\delta K\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

This
completes the proof of
Proposition
3.10
.

\square

Proof of
Proposition

3.11
:

Let
\bar{f}\in\mathcal{F}
and
c>0
. By assumption,

\mathcal{I}
is a well-behaved set of
support-extending interventions on
X
.
Since
\mathrm{supp}_{\mathcal{I}}^{M}(X)\setminus\mathrm{supp}^{M}(X)
has non-empty
interior, there exists an intervention
i_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
and
\varepsilon>0

such that
\mathbb{P}_{M(i_{0})}(X\in B)\geq\varepsilon
,
for some open subset

B\subsetneq\bar{B}
, such that
\mathrm{dist}(B,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bar{B})>0
, where
\bar{B}:=\mathrm{supp}_{\mathcal{I}}^{M}(X)\setminus\mathrm{supp}^{M}(X)
.
Let
\tilde{f}
be any continuous
function satisfying that, for all
x\in B\cup(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bar{B})
,

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(x)=\begin{cases}\bar{f}(x)+\gamma,\quad&x\in B\\
f(x),\quad&x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bar{B},\end{cases}

where

\gamma:=\varepsilon^{-1/2}\left\{(2\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}}[\xi_{Y}^{2}]+c)^{1/2}+(\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}}[\xi_{Y}^{2}])^{1/2}\right\}
.

Consider a secondary model

\tilde{M}=(\tilde{f},g,h_{1},h_{2},Q)\in\mathcal{M}
. Then, by
Assumption
3.1
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{M}=\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{M}}
. Since
\mathcal{I}
only consists of
interventions on
X
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}_{M(i_{0})}(X\in B)=\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{M}(i_{0})}(X\in B)
(this
holds since all components of
\tilde{M}
and
M
are equal, except for the
function
f
, which is not allowed to enter in the intervention on

X
). Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{0})}\big[(Y-\bar{f}(X))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{0})}\big[(Y-\bar{f}(X))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(X)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{0})}\big[(\gamma+\xi_{Y})^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(X)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{2}\varepsilon+2\gamma\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{0})}\big[\xi_{Y}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(X)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{2}\varepsilon-2\gamma\left(\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}\big]\varepsilon\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=c+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}}[\xi_{Y}^{2}],

(B.33)

where the third inequality follows from Cauchy–Schwarz.
Further, by the definition of the infimum it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]\leq\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-\tilde{f}(X))^{2}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}}[\xi_{Y}^{2}].\end{split}

(B.34)

Therefore, combining (
B.46
) and (
B.34
), the
claim follows.

\square

Proof of
Proposition

3.12
:

We prove the result by showing that under
Assumption
3.3
it is possible to express
interventions on
A
as confounding-preserving interventions on
X

and applying
Propositions
3.7
and
3.8
. To
avoid confusion, we will throughout this proof denote the true model
by
M^{0}=(f^{0},g^{0},h_{1}^{0},h_{2}^{0},Q^{0})
. Fix an intervention

i\in\mathcal{I}
. Since it is an intervention on
A
, there exist
\psi^{i}

and
I^{i}
such that for any
M=(f,g,h_{1},h_{2},Q)\in\mathcal{M}
, the
intervened SCM
M(i)
is of the form

\displaystyle A^{i}

\displaystyle:=\psi^{i}(I^{i},\varepsilon_{A}^{i}),\quad H^{i}:=\varepsilon_{H}^{i},

\displaystyle X^{i}

\displaystyle:=g(A^{i})+h_{2}(H^{i},\varepsilon_{X}^{i}),

\displaystyle Y^{i}

\displaystyle:=f(X^{i})+h_{1}(H^{i},\varepsilon_{Y}^{i}),

where
(\varepsilon^{i}_{X},\varepsilon^{i}_{Y},\varepsilon^{i}_{A},\varepsilon^{i}_{H})\sim Q
. We now define
a confounding-preserving intervention
j
on
X
, such that, for all
models
\tilde{M}
with
\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{M}}=\mathbb{P}_{M}
, the distribution of

(X,Y)
under
\tilde{M}(j)
coincides with that under

\tilde{M}(i)
. To that end, define the intervention function

\bar{\psi}^{j}(h_{2},A^{j},H^{j},\varepsilon^{j}_{X},I^{j})\coloneqq g^{0}(\psi^{i}(I^{j},A^{j}))+h_{2}(H^{j},\varepsilon_{X}^{j}),

where
g^{0}
is the fixed function corresponding to model
M
, and
therefore not an argument of
\bar{\psi}^{j}
. Let now
j
be
the intervention on
X
satisfying that, for all

M=(f,g,h_{1},h_{2},Q)\in\mathcal{M}
, the intervened model
M(j)
is
given as

\displaystyle A^{j}:=\varepsilon_{A}^{j},\quad H^{j}:=\varepsilon_{H}^{j},

\displaystyle X^{j}:=\bar{\psi}^{j}(h_{2},A^{j},H^{j},\varepsilon^{j}_{X},I^{j}),

\displaystyle Y^{j}:=f(X^{j})+h_{1}(H^{j},\varepsilon_{Y}^{j}),

where
(\varepsilon^{j}_{X},\varepsilon^{j}_{Y},\varepsilon^{j}_{A},\varepsilon^{j}_{H})\sim Q
and where
I^{j}

is chosen such that
I^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}I^{i}
. By definition,
j
is
a confounding-preserving intervention. Let now

\tilde{M}=(\tilde{f},\tilde{g},\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2},\tilde{Q})
be such that
\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{M}}=\mathbb{P}_{M}
, and let

(\tilde{X}^{i},\tilde{Y}^{i})
and
(\tilde{X}^{j},\tilde{Y}^{j})
be
generated under
\tilde{M}(i)
and
\tilde{M}(j)
, respectively. By
Assumption
3.3
, it holds for all

a\in\mathrm{supp}(A)\cup\mathrm{supp}_{\mathcal{I}}(A)
that
\tilde{g}(a)=g^{0}(a)
.
Hence, we get that

\displaystyle(\tilde{X}^{i},\tilde{Y}^{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(\tilde{g}(\psi^{i}(I^{i},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{A}^{i}))+\tilde{h}_{2}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{i}_{H},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{X}^{i}),\tilde{f}(\tilde{g}(\psi^{i}(I^{i},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{A}^{i}))

\displaystyle+\tilde{h}_{2}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{i}_{H},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{X}^{i}))+\tilde{h}_{1}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{H}^{i},\tilde{\varepsilon}^{i}_{Y}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(g^{0}(\psi^{i}(I^{i},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{A}^{i}))+\tilde{h}_{2}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{i}_{H},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{X}^{i}),\tilde{f}(g^{0}(\psi^{i}(I^{i},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{A}^{i}))

\displaystyle+\tilde{h}_{2}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{i}_{H},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{X}^{i}))+\tilde{h}_{1}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{H}^{i},\tilde{\varepsilon}^{i}_{Y}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(g^{0}(\psi^{i}(I^{j},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{A}^{j}))+\tilde{h}_{2}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{j}_{H},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{X}^{j}),\tilde{f}(g^{0}(\psi^{i}(I^{j},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{A}^{j}))

\displaystyle+\tilde{h}_{2}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{j}_{H},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{X}^{j}))+\tilde{h}_{1}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{H}^{j},\tilde{\varepsilon}^{j}_{Y}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(\bar{\psi}^{j}(\tilde{h}_{2},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{A}^{j},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{H}^{j},\tilde{\varepsilon}^{j}_{X},I^{j}),\tilde{f}(\bar{\psi}^{j}(\tilde{h}_{2},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{A}^{j},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{H}^{j},\tilde{\varepsilon}^{j}_{X},I^{j}))

\displaystyle+\tilde{h}_{1}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{H}^{j},\tilde{\varepsilon}^{j}_{Y}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(\tilde{X}^{j},\tilde{Y}^{j}),

as desired. Since
i\in\mathcal{I}
was arbitrary, we have now shown that
there exists a mapping
\pi
from
\mathcal{I}
into a set
\mathcal{J}
of
confounding-preserving (and hence a well-behaved set) of
interventions on
X
, such that for all
\tilde{M}
with

\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{M}}=\mathbb{P}_{M}
,

\mathbb{P}^{(X,Y)}_{\tilde{M}(i)}=\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{M}(\pi(i))}^{(X,Y)}
. Hence,
we can rewrite Equation (
3.3
) in
Definition
3.1
in terms of the set
\mathcal{J}
. The
result now follows from
Propositions
3.7
and
3.8
.

\square

Proof of
Proposition

3.13
:

Let
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be such that
f(x)=b^{\top}x
for all

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We start by characterizing the error

\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]
. Let us consider models of the form

\tilde{M}=(f,\tilde{g},h_{1},h_{2},Q)\in\mathcal{M}
for some function

\tilde{g}\in\mathcal{G}
with
\tilde{g}(a)=g(a)
for all

a\in\mathrm{supp}_{M}(A)
. Clearly, any such model satisfies that

\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{M}}=\mathbb{P}_{M}
. For every
a\in\mathcal{A}
, let

i_{a}\in\mathcal{I}
denote the corresponding hard intervention on
A
.
For every
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
b_{\diamond}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we then have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{a})}\big[(Y-b_{\diamond}^{\top}X)^{2}\big]

(B.35)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{a})}\big[(b^{\top}X+\xi_{Y}-b_{\diamond}^{\top}X)^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(b-b_{\diamond})^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{a})}[XX^{\top}](b-b_{\diamond})

\displaystyle+2(b-b_{\diamond})^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{a})}[X\xi_{Y}]+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{a})}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(b-b_{\diamond})^{\top}\underbrace{(\tilde{g}(a)\tilde{g}(a)^{\top}+\mathbb{E}_{M}[\xi_{X}\xi_{X}^{\top}])}_{=:K_{\tilde{M}}(a)}(b-b_{\diamond})

\displaystyle+2(b-b_{\diamond})^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{M}[\xi_{X}\xi_{Y}]+\mathbb{E}_{M}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}],

where we have used that, under
i_{a}
, the distribution of

(\xi_{X},\xi_{Y})

is unaffected.
We now show that, for any
\tilde{M}
with the above form, the causal
function
f
does not minimize the worst-case
risk
across interventions
in
\mathcal{I}
. The idea is to show that the worst-case risk (
B.35
)
strictly decreases at

b_{\diamond}=b
in the direction

u:=\mathbb{E}_{M}[\xi_{X}\xi_{Y}]/\lVert\mathbb{E}_{M}[\xi_{X}\xi_{Y}]\rVert_{2}
. For every

a\in\mathcal{A}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, define

\displaystyle\ell_{\tilde{M},a}(s):

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{a})}\big[(Y-(b+su)^{\top}X)^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=u^{\top}K_{\tilde{M}}(a)u\cdot s^{2}-2u^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{M}[\xi_{X}\xi_{Y}]\cdot s+\mathbb{E}_{M}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}].

For every
a
,

\ell_{\tilde{M},a}^{\prime}(0)=-2\lVert\mathbb{E}_{M}[\xi_{X}\xi_{Y}]\rVert_{2}<0
,
showing that
\ell_{\tilde{M},a}
is strictly decreasing at
s=0

(with a derivative that is bounded away from 0 across all
a\in\mathcal{A}
).
By boundedness of
\mathcal{A}
and by the continuity of
a\mapsto\ell_{\tilde{M},a}^{\prime\prime}(0)=2u^{\top}K_{\tilde{M}}(a)u
,
it further follows that
\sup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\lvert\ell^{\prime\prime}_{\tilde{M},a}(0)\rvert<\infty
.
Hence, we can find
s_{0}>0
such that for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
\ell_{\tilde{M},a}(0)>\ell_{\tilde{M},a}(s_{0})
.
It now follows by continuity of
(a,s)\mapsto\ell_{\tilde{M},a}(s)
that

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-b^{\top}X)^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=\sup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\ell_{\tilde{M},a}(0)

\displaystyle>\sup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\ell_{\tilde{M},a}(s_{0})

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-(b+s_{0}u)^{\top}X)^{2}\big],

showing that
b+s_{0}u
attains a lower worst-case risk than
b
.

We now show that all functions other than
f
may result in an
arbitrarily large error. Let
\bar{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{b\}
be
given, and let
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
be such that

b_{j}\neq\bar{b}_{j}
. The idea is to construct a function

\tilde{g}\in\mathcal{G}
such that, under the corresponding model

\tilde{M}=(f,\tilde{g},h_{1},h_{2},Q)\in\mathcal{M}
, some hard
interventions on
A
result in strong shifts of the
j
th coordinate
of
X
. Let
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Let
e_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the
j
th unit vector, and assume
that
\tilde{g}(a)=ne_{j}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Using
(
B.35
), it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{a})}\big[(Y-\bar{b}^{\top}X)^{2}\big]=

\displaystyle n^{2}(\bar{b}_{j}-b_{j})^{2}+(\bar{b}-b)^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{M}[\xi_{X}\xi_{X}^{\top}](\bar{b}-b)

\displaystyle+2(\bar{b}-b)^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{M}[\xi_{X}\xi_{Y}]+\mathbb{E}_{M}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}].

By letting
n\to\infty
, we see that the above error may become
arbitrarily large. Given any
c>0
, we can therefore construct

\tilde{g}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{a})}\big[(Y-\bar{b}^{\top}X)^{2}\big]\geq c+\mathbb{E}_{M}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}]
. By
carefully
choosing

a\in\text{int}(\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathrm{supp}_{M}(A))
, this can be done
such that
\tilde{g}
is continuous and
\tilde{g}(a)=g(a)

for all
a\in\mathrm{supp}_{M}(A)
, ensuring that
\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{M}}=\mathbb{P}_{M}
. It follows that

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{a})}\big[(Y-\bar{b}^{\top}X)^{2}\big]-\mathbb{E}_{M}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{a})}\big[(Y-\bar{b}^{\top}X)^{2}\big]-\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-b^{\top}X)^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i_{a})}\big[(Y-\bar{b}^{\top}X)^{2}\big]-\inf_{b_{\diamond}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-b_{\diamond}^{\top}X)^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-\bar{b}^{\top}X)^{2}\big]-\inf_{b_{\diamond}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-b_{\diamond}^{\top}X)^{2}],

which completes the proof of Proposition
3.13
.

\square

Proof of
Proposition

3.14
:

By assumption,
\mathcal{I}
is a set of
interventions on
X
or
A
of which at least one is confounding-removing.
Now fix any

\tilde{M}=(f_{\eta_{0}}(x;\tilde{\theta}),\tilde{g},\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2},\tilde{Q})\in\mathcal{M},

with
\mathbb{P}_{M}=\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{M}}
.
By Proposition
3.1
, we have that a minimax solution is given by the causal function. That is,

\displaystyle\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}_{\eta_{0}}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\tilde{\theta}))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{M}[\xi^{2}_{Y}],

where we used that
\xi_{Y}
is unaffected by an intervention on
X
.
By the support restriction
\mathrm{supp}^{M}(X)\subseteq(a,b)
we know that

\displaystyle f_{\eta_{0}}(x;\theta^{0})

\displaystyle=B(x)^{\top}\theta^{0},

\displaystyle f_{\eta_{0}}(x;\tilde{\theta})

\displaystyle=B(x)^{\top}\tilde{\theta},

\displaystyle f_{\eta_{0}}(x;\hat{\theta}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}^{n})

\displaystyle=B(x)^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}^{n},

for all
x\in\mathrm{supp}^{M}(X)
. Furthermore, as
Y=B(X)^{\top}\theta^{0}+\xi_{Y}

\mathbb{P}_{M}
-almost surely, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{M}\left[C(A)Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{M}\left[C(A)B(X)^{\top}\theta^{0}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{M}\left[C(A)\xi_{Y}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{M}\left[C(A)B(X)^{\top}\right]\theta^{0},

(B.36)

where we used the assumptions that
\mathbb{E}\left[\xi_{Y}\right]=0
and
A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\xi_{Y}
by the exogeneity of
A
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}}\left[C(A)Y\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}}\left[C(A)B(X)^{\top}\right]\tilde{\theta}.

As
\mathbb{P}_{M}=\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{M}}
, we have that
\mathbb{E}_{M}[C(A)Y]=\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}}[C(A)Y]
and
\mathbb{E}_{M}[C(A)B(X)^{\top}]=\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}}[C(A)B(X)^{\top}]
, hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{M}\left[C(A)B(X)^{\top}\right]\tilde{\theta}=\mathbb{E}_{M}\left[C(A)B(X)^{\top}\right]\theta^{0}\iff\tilde{\theta}=\theta^{0},

by assumption
(B2)
,
which states that
\mathbb{E}[C(A)B(X)^{\top}]
is of full rank
(bijective).
In other words, the causal function parameterized by

\theta^{0}
is identified from the observational distribution. Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
are therefore satisfied.
Furthermore, we also have that

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\big\{\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\theta^{0})-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}))^{2}\big]+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\quad+2\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[\xi_{Y}(f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\theta^{0})-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}))\big]\big\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\big\{\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\theta^{0})-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}))^{2}\big]+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\quad+2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}\big]\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\theta^{0})-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}))^{2}\big]}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\theta^{0})-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}))^{2}\big]+\mathbb{E}_{M}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\quad+2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{M}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}\big]\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\theta^{0})-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}))^{2}\big]},

by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, where we additionally used that

\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}[\xi_{Y}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{M}[\xi_{Y}^{2}]
as
\xi_{Y}
is
unaffected by interventions on
X
. Thus,

\displaystyle\big|\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}))^{2}\big]-\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}_{\eta_{0}}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\theta^{0})-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\qquad+2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{M}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}\big]\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\theta^{0})-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}))^{2}\big]}.

For the next few derivations let
\hat{\theta}=\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}
for notational simplicity. Note that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle(f_{\eta_{0}}(x;\theta^{0})-f_{\eta_{0}}(x;\hat{\theta}))^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\,(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})^{\top}B(x)B(x)^{\top}(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})

\displaystyle+(B(a)^{\top}(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})+B^{\prime}(a)^{\top}(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})(x-a))^{2}

\displaystyle+(B(b)^{\top}(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})+B^{\prime}(b)^{\top}(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})(x-b))^{2}.

The second term has the following upper bound

\displaystyle(B(

\displaystyle a)^{\top}(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})+B^{\prime}(a)^{\top}(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})(x-a))^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})^{\top}B(a)B(a)^{\top}(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})

\displaystyle+(x-a)^{2}(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})^{\top}B^{\prime}(a)B^{\prime}(a)^{\top}(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})

\displaystyle+2(x-a)(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})^{\top}B^{\prime}(a)B(a)^{\top}(\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}(B(a)B(a)^{\top})\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+(x-a)^{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}(B^{\prime}(a)B^{\prime}(a)^{\top})\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+(x-a)\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}(B^{\prime}(a)B(a)^{\top}+B(a)B^{\prime}(a)^{\top})\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2},

where
\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}
denotes the maximum eigenvalue. An analogous upper bound can be constructed for the third term.
Thus, by combining these two upper bounds with a similar upper bound for the first term, we arrive at

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

{}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\theta^{0})-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}))^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}(\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}[B(X)B(X)^{\top}])\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}(B(a)B(a)^{\top})\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}[(X-a)^{2}]\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}(B^{\prime}(a)B^{\prime}(a)^{\top})\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}[X-a]\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}(B^{\prime}(a)B(a)^{\top}+B(a)B^{\prime}(a)^{\top})\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}(B(b)B(b)^{\top})\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}[(X-b)^{2}]\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}(B^{\prime}(b)B^{\prime}(b)^{\top})\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}[X-b]\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}(B^{\prime}(b)B(b)^{\top}+B(b)B^{\prime}(b)^{\top})\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}.

Assumption
(B1)

imposes that
\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}[X^{2}]

and
\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\lambda_{\mathrm{m}}(\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}[B(X)B(X)^{\top}])
are finite. Hence, the supremum of each of
the above terms is finite. That is, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\eta_{0}}(X;\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}))^{2}\big]-\inf_{f_{\diamond}\in\mathcal{F}_{\eta_{0}}}\,\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{M}(i)}\big[(Y-f_{\diamond}(X))^{2}\big]\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq c\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}\|_{2}^{2}+2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{M}\big[\xi_{Y}^{2}\big]c}\|\theta^{0}-\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}\|_{2}.

It therefore suffices to show that

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}\underset{n\to\infty}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\theta^{0},

with respect to the distribution induced by
M
. To simplify
notation, we henceforth drop the
M
subscript in the expectations
and probabilities.
Note that by the rank conditions in
(B2)
, and the law of large numbers, we may assume that the corresponding sample product moments satisfy the same conditions. That is, for the
purpose of the following arguments, it suffices that the sample product moment only satisfies these rank
conditions asymptotically with probability one.

Let
B:=B(X)
,
C:=C(A)
, let

\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{C}
be row-wise stacked i.i.d. copies of
B(X)^{\top}
and

C(A)^{\top}
, and recall the definition

\mathbf{P}_{\delta}:=\mathbf{C}\left(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}+\delta\mathbf{M}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{C}^{\top}
. By convexity of the objective function we can find a closed form expression for our estimator of
\theta^{0}
by solving the corresponding normal equations. The closed form expression is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda,\eta,\mu}:

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\lVert\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\theta\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lambda\lVert\mathbf{P}_{\delta}(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\theta)\rVert_{2}^{2}+\gamma\theta^{\top}\mathbf{K}\theta

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}}{n}+\lambda^{\star}_{n}\frac{\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\delta}\mathbf{P}_{\delta}\mathbf{B}}{n}+\frac{\gamma\mathbf{K}}{n}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}}{n}+\lambda^{\star}_{n}\frac{\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\delta}\mathbf{P}_{\delta}\mathbf{Y}}{n}\right),

where we used that
\lambda^{\star}_{n}\in[0,\infty)
almost surely by
(C2)
.
Consequently
(using standard convergence arguments and that
n^{-1}\gamma\mathbf{K}
and
n^{-1}\delta\mathbf{M}
converges to zero in
probability),
if
\lambda^{\star}_{n}
diverges to infinity in probability as
n
tends to infinity, then

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[BC^{\top}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[CC^{\top}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[CB^{\top}\right]\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[BC^{\top}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[CC^{\top}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[CY\right]

\displaystyle=\theta^{0}.

Here, we also used that the terms multiplied by
\lambda^{\star}_{n}
are the only asymptotically relevant terms.
These are the standard arguments that the K-class estimator (with minor penalized regression modifications) is consistent as long as the parameter
\lambda^{\star}_{n}
converges to infinity, or, equivalently,
\kappa_{n}^{\star}=\lambda^{\star}_{n}/(1+\lambda^{\star}_{n})
converges to one in probability.

We now consider two cases:
(i)

\mathbb{E}[B\xi_{Y}]\not=0
and
(ii)

\mathbb{E}[B\xi_{Y}]=0
, corresponding to the case with unmeasured confounding and without, respectively. For
(i)
we show that
\lambda^{\star}_{n}
converges to infinity in probability and for
(ii)
we show consistency by other means (as
\lambda^{\star}_{n}
might not converge to infinity in this case).

Case (i):
The confounded case
\mathbb{E}[B\xi_{Y}]\not=0
. It suffices to show that

\lambda^{\star}_{n}:=\inf\{\lambda\geq 0:T_{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu})\leq q(\alpha)\}\underset{n\to\infty}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\infty.

To that end, note that for fixed
\lambda\geq 0
we have
that

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu}

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\theta_{\lambda},

(B.37)

where

\displaystyle\theta_{\lambda}\,

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathbb{E}\left[BB^{\top}\right]+\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[BC^{\top}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[CC^{\top}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[CB^{\top}\right]\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\mathbb{E}\left[BY\right]+\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[BC^{\top}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[CC^{\top}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[CY\right]\right).

(B.38)

Recall that (
B.36
) states that
\mathbb{E}\left[CY\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[CB^{\top}\right]\theta^{0}
.
Using (
B.36
) and that
Y=B^{\top}\theta^{0}+\xi_{Y}

\mathbb{P}_{M}
-almost surely, we have that the latter factor of (
B.38
) is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[BY\right]+\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[BC^{\top}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[CC^{\top}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[CY\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}\left[BB^{\top}\right]\theta^{0}+\mathbb{E}\left[B\xi_{Y}\right]+\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[BC^{\top}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[CC^{\top}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[CB^{\top}\right]\theta^{0}

\displaystyle\quad=\left(\mathbb{E}\left[BB^{\top}\right]+\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[BC^{\top}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[CC^{\top}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[CB^{\top}\right]\right)\theta^{0}+\mathbb{E}\left[B\xi_{Y}\right].

Inserting this into (
B.38
) we arrive at the following representation of
\theta_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\theta_{\lambda}=\theta^{0}+\left(\mathbb{E}\left[BB^{\top}\right]+\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[BC^{\top}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[CC^{\top}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[CB^{\top}\right]\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[B\xi_{Y}\right].

(B.39)

Since
\mathbb{E}\left[B\xi_{Y}\right]\not=0
by assumption, the above yields that

\displaystyle\forall\lambda\geq 0:\quad\quad\theta^{0}\not=\theta_{\lambda}.

(B.40)

Now we prove that
\lambda^{\star}_{n}
diverges to infinity in probability as
n
tends to infinity. That is, for any
\lambda\geq 0
we will prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\lambda^{\star}_{n}\leq\lambda)=0.

We fix an arbitrary
\lambda\geq 0
. By
(
B.40
) we have that

\theta^{0}\not=\theta_{\lambda}
. This implies that there exists an

\varepsilon>0
such that
\theta^{0}\not\in\overline{B(\theta_{\lambda},\varepsilon)}
, where
\overline{B(\theta_{\lambda},\varepsilon)}
is the
closed ball in

\mathbb{R}^{k}
with center
\theta_{\lambda}
and radius
\varepsilon
.
By the consistency result (
B.37
), we know that
the sequence of events
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, given by

A_{n}:=(|\hat{\theta}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu}^{n}-\theta_{\lambda}|\leq\varepsilon)=(\hat{\theta}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu}^{n}\in\overline{B(\theta_{\lambda},\varepsilon)}),

satisfies
\mathbb{P}(A_{n})\to 1
as
n\to\infty
.
By assumption
(C3)
we have that

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}\mapsto T_{n}(\theta^{n}_{\tilde{\lambda},\eta_{0},\mu}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\theta\mapsto T_{n}(\theta),

are weakly decreasing and continuous, respectively.
Together with the continuity of
\tilde{\lambda}\mapsto\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{\lambda},\eta_{0},\mu}^{n}
,
this implies that also the mapping
\tilde{\lambda}\mapsto T_{n}(\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{\lambda},\eta_{0},\mu}^{n})

is continuous. It now follows from Assumption
(C2)

(stating that
\lambda^{\star}_{n}
is almost surely finite) that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbb{P}(T_{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu})\leq q(\alpha))=1
. Furthermore, since
\tilde{\lambda}\mapsto T_{n}(\theta^{n}_{\tilde{\lambda},\eta_{0},\mu})

is weakly decreasing, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\lambda^{\star}_{n}\leq\lambda)=

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{P}(\{\lambda^{\star}_{n}\leq\lambda\}\cap\{T_{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu})\leq q(\alpha)\})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{P}(\{\lambda^{\star}_{n}\leq\lambda\}\cap\{T_{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu})\leq q(\alpha)\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{P}(\{\lambda^{\star}_{n}\leq\lambda\}\cap\{T_{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu})\leq q(\alpha)\}\cap A_{n})

\displaystyle\qquad+\,\,\mathbb{P}(\{\lambda^{\star}_{n}\leq\lambda\}\cap\{T_{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu})\leq q(\alpha)\}\cap A_{n}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{P}(\{\lambda^{\star}_{n}\leq\lambda\}\cap\{T_{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu})\leq q(\alpha)\}\cap\{|\hat{\theta}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu}^{n}-\theta_{\lambda}|\leq\varepsilon\})

\displaystyle\qquad+\,\,\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{c}).

It now suffices to show that the first term converges to zero, since
\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{c})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
We have

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{P}(\{\lambda^{\star}_{n}\leq\lambda\}\cap\{T_{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu})\leq q(\alpha)\}\cap\{|\hat{\theta}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu}^{n}-\theta_{\lambda}|\leq\varepsilon\})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\{\lambda^{\star}_{n}\leq\lambda\}\cap\Big\{\inf_{\theta\in\overline{B(\theta_{\lambda},\varepsilon)}}T_{n}(\theta)\leq q(\alpha)\Big\}\cap\{|\hat{\theta}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu}^{n}-\theta_{\lambda}|\leq\varepsilon\}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\inf_{\theta\in\overline{B(\theta_{\lambda},\varepsilon)}}T_{n}(\theta)\leq q(\alpha)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0,

as
n\to\infty
, since
\overline{B(\theta_{\lambda},\varepsilon)}
is a compact set not containing
\theta^{0}
. Here, we used that the test statistic
(T_{n})
is assumed to have compact uniform power
(C1)
.
Hence,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\lambda^{\star}_{n}\leq\lambda)=0
for any
\lambda\geq 0
,
proving that
\lambda^{\star}_{n}
diverges to infinity in probability, which ensures consistency.

Case (ii):
the unconfounded case
\mathbb{E}[B(X)\xi_{Y}]=0
. Recall that

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu}\,

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\lVert\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\theta\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lambda\lVert\mathbf{P}_{\delta}(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\theta)\rVert_{2}^{2}+\gamma\theta^{\top}\mathbf{K}\theta

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\theta)+\lambda l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\theta)+\gamma l_{\text{PEN}}(\theta),

(B.41)

where we defined
l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\theta):=n^{-1}\lVert\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\theta\rVert_{2}^{2}
,
l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\theta):=n^{-1}\lVert\mathbf{P}_{\delta}(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\theta)\rVert_{2}^{2}
, and
l_{\text{PEN}}(\theta):=n^{-1}\theta^{\top}\mathbf{K}\theta
.
For any
0\leq\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
we have

\displaystyle l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{1},\eta_{0},\mu})+\lambda_{1}l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{1},\eta_{0},\mu})+\gamma l_{\text{PEN}}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{1},\eta_{0},\mu})

\displaystyle\quad\leq l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{2},\eta_{0},\mu})+\lambda_{1}l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{2},\eta_{0},\mu})+\gamma l_{\text{PEN}}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{2},\eta_{0},\mu})

\displaystyle\quad=l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{2},\eta_{0},\mu})+\lambda_{2}l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{2},\eta_{0},\mu})+\gamma l_{\text{PEN}}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{2},\eta_{0},\mu})

\displaystyle\qquad+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{2},\eta_{0},\mu})

\displaystyle\quad\leq l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{1},\eta_{0},\mu})+\lambda_{2}l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{1},\eta_{0},\mu})+\gamma l_{\text{PEN}}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{1},\eta_{0},\mu})

\displaystyle\qquad+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{2},\eta_{0},\mu}),

where we used (
B.41
). Rearranging this inequality and dividing by
(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})
yields

\displaystyle l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{1},\eta_{0},\mu})\geq l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda_{2},\eta_{0},\mu}),

proving that
\lambda\mapsto l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda,\eta_{0},\mu})
is weakly decreasing. Thus, since
\lambda^{\star}_{n}\geq 0
almost surely, we have that

\displaystyle l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu})\leq\,l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{0,\eta_{0},\mu})=\,n^{-1}(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\hat{\theta}^{n}_{0,\eta_{0},\mu})^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\delta}\mathbf{P}_{\delta}(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\hat{\theta}^{n}_{0,\eta_{0},\mu}).

(B.42)

Furthermore, recall from (
B.37
) that

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{n}_{0,\eta_{0},\mu}\underset{n\to\infty}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\theta_{0}=\theta^{0},

(B.43)

where the last equality follows from (
B.39
) using that we are in the unconfounded case
\mathbb{E}[B(X)\xi_{Y}]=0
. By expanding and deriving convergence statements for each term, we get

\displaystyle(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\hat{\theta}^{n}_{0,\eta_{0},\mu})^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{\delta}\mathbf{P}_{\delta}(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\hat{\theta}^{n}_{0,\eta_{0},\mu})

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}}(\mathbb{E}[YC^{\top}]-\theta_{0}\mathbb{E}[BC^{\top}])\mathbb{E}[C^{\top}C]^{-1}(\mathbb{E}[CY]-\mathbb{E}[CB^{\top}]\theta_{0})

\displaystyle=0,

(B.44)

where we used Slutsky’s theorem, the weak law of large numbers, (
B.43
) and (
B.36
). Thus, by (
B.42
) and (
B.44
) it holds that

\displaystyle l_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu})=n^{-1}\|\mathbf{P}_{\delta}(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu})\|_{2}^{2}\underset{n\to\infty}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}}0.

For any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{\delta}z\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=z^{\top}\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}+\delta\mathbf{M})^{-1}\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}+\delta\mathbf{M})^{-1}\mathbf{C}^{\top}z

\displaystyle=z^{\top}\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}+\delta\mathbf{M})^{-1}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C})^{1/2}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C})^{1/2}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}+\delta\mathbf{M})^{-1}\mathbf{C}^{\top}z

\displaystyle=\|(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C})^{1/2}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}+\delta\mathbf{M})^{-1}\mathbf{C}^{\top}z\|_{2}^{2},

hence

\displaystyle\lVert H_{n}-G_{n}\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|n^{-1/2}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C})^{1/2}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}+\delta\mathbf{M})^{-1}\mathbf{C}^{\top}(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{B}\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0,

(B.45)

where for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
G_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
H_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 1}
are defined as

\displaystyle G_{n}

\displaystyle:=n^{-1/2}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C})^{1/2}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}+\delta\mathbf{M})^{-1}\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{B},\text{ and }

\displaystyle H_{n}

\displaystyle:=n^{-1/2}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C})^{1/2}(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}+\delta\mathbf{M})^{-1}\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}.

Using the weak law of large numbers, the continuous mapping theorem and Slutsky’s theorem,
it follows that, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle G_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}G

\displaystyle:=E[CC^{\top}]^{1/2}E[CC^{\top}]^{-1}E[CB^{\top}],\text{ and }

\displaystyle H_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}H

\displaystyle:=E[CC^{\top}]^{1/2}E[CC^{\top}]^{-1}E[CY]

\displaystyle=E[CC^{\top}]^{1/2}E[CC^{\top}]^{-1}E[CB^{\top}]\theta^{0}

\displaystyle=G\theta^{0},

where the second to last equality follows from (
B.36
).
Together with (
B.45
), we now have that

\displaystyle\,\lVert G_{n}\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}-G\theta^{0}\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\lVert G_{n}\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}-H_{n}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert H_{n}-G\theta^{0}\rVert_{2}^{2}\underset{n\to\infty}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}}0.

Furthermore, by the rank assumptions in
(B2)
we have that
G_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is of full rank (with probability tending to one), hence

\displaystyle\|\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}-\theta^{0}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|G_{n}^{-1}G_{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}-\theta^{0})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|G_{n}^{-1}\|_{\text{op}}^{2}\|G_{n}(\hat{\theta}^{n}_{\lambda^{\star}_{n},\eta_{0},\mu}-\theta^{0})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}\|G^{-1}\|_{\text{op}}^{2}\cdot 0

\displaystyle=0,

as
n\to\infty
, proving the proposition.

\square

Appendix C
Structure Learning For Directed Trees

C.47 Graph Terminology

C.48

C.48 Further Details on Section

C.49 Further Details on the Simulation Experiments

B.46

B.46 Proofs

C.47
Graph Terminology

A
directed graph

\mathcal{G}=(V,\mathcal{E})
consists of
p\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
vertices (nodes)
V=\{1,\ldots,p\}
and a collection of directed edges
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\{(j\to i)\equiv(j,i):i,j\in V,i\not=j\}
. For any graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,\mathcal{E})
we let
{\mathrm{pa}^{\mathcal{G}}({i})}:=\{v\in V:\exists(v,i)\in\mathcal{E}\}
and
{\mathrm{ch}^{\mathcal{G}}({i})}:=\{v\in V:\exists(j,v)\in\mathcal{E}\}
denote the
parents
and
children
of node
i\in V
and we define root nodes
\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G}):=\{v\in V:{\mathrm{pa}^{\mathcal{G}}({i})}=\emptyset\}
as nodes with no parents (that is, no incoming edges).
A
path
in
\mathcal{G}
between two nodes
i_{1},i_{k}\in V
consists of a sequence
(i_{1},i_{2}),\ldots,(i_{k-1},i_{k})
of pairs of nodes such that for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}
, we have either

(i_{j}\to i_{j+1})\in\mathcal{E}

or

(i_{j+1}\to i_{j})\in\mathcal{E}
.
A
directed path
in
\mathcal{G}
between two nodes
i_{1},i_{k}\in V
consists of a sequence
(i_{1},i_{2}),\ldots,(i_{k-1},i_{k})
of pairs of nodes such that for all

j\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}
, we have
(i_{j}\to i_{j+1})\in\mathcal{E}
. Furthermore, we let
{\mathrm{an}^{\mathcal{G}}({i})}
and
{\mathrm{de}^{\mathcal{G}}({i})}
denote the
ancestors
and
descendants
of node
i\in V
, consisting of all nodes
j\in V
for which there exists a directed path to and from
i
, respectively. We let
{\mathrm{nd}^{\mathcal{G}}({i})}
denote the
non-descendants
of
i
. A
directed acyclic graph
(DAG) is a directed graph that does not contain any directed cycles, i.e., directed paths visiting the same node twice.
We say that a graph is
connected
if a (possibly undirected) path exists between any two nodes. A
directed tree
is a connected DAG in which all nodes have at most one parent. More specifically, every node has a unique parent except the root node, which has no parent. The root node
\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})
is the unique node such there exists a directed path from
\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})
to any other node in the directed tree. In graph theory, a directed tree is also called an
arborescence
, a
directed rooted tree
, and a
rooted out-tree
. A graph
\mathcal{G}=(V^{\prime},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
is a
subgraph
of another graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,\mathcal{E})
if
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
,
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
and for all
(j\to i)\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
it holds that
j,i\in V^{\prime}
. A subgraph is
spanning
if
V^{\prime}=V
. For any DAG
\mathcal{G}=(V,\mathcal{E})
and three mutually distinct subsets
A,B,C\subseteq V
we let
A{\perp\!\!\!\!\perp_{\mathcal{G}}}B\,|\,C
denote that
A
and
B
are d-separated by
C
in
\mathcal{G}

(
Pearl 2009
, see, e.g.,)
.

C.48
Further Details on Section
4.22

C.1
.

The conditional entropy score gap is not strictly positive when considering the alternative graphs
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
that are Markov equivalent to the causal graph
\mathcal{G}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\mathrm{MEC}(\mathcal{G})
. A simple translation of the conditional entropy score function reveals that

\displaystyle\ell_{\mathrm{CE}}(\tilde{\mathcal{G}})+C=\sum_{(j\to i)\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}h(X_{i}|X_{j})-h(X_{i})=-\sum_{(j\to i)\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}I(X_{i};X_{j}),

for a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}
. By symmetry of the mutual information, it holds that
\ell_{\mathrm{CE}}(\tilde{\mathcal{G}})=\ell_{\mathrm{CE}}(\mathcal{G})
,
for any
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\mathrm{MEC}(\mathcal{G})
, since

\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathcal{G}
share the same skeleton. Thus, the conditional entropy score function can, at most, identify the Markov equivalence class of the causal graph. In fact, the polytree causal structure learning method of
Rebane and Pearl 1987
uses the above translated conditional entropy score function to recover the skeleton of the causal graph.

C.1
Negative local Gaussian score gap.

Consider two graphs
\mathcal{G}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
with different root nodes, i.e.,
\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})\not=\mathrm{rt}(\tilde{\mathcal{G}})
. If
x\mapsto\mathbb{E}[X_{\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})}|X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})})}}=x]
is not almost surely constant, then it holds that

\displaystyle\ell_{\mathrm{G}}(\tilde{\mathcal{G}},\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G}))-\ell_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathcal{G},\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G}))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(X_{\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})}-\mathbb{E}[X_{\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})}|X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})})}}])^{2}]-\mathrm{Var}(X_{\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(X_{\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})}|X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})})}})]-\mathrm{Var}(X_{\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})})

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{Var}(\mathbb{E}[X_{\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})}|X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})})}}])<0.

C.49
Further Details on the Simulation Experiments

This section contains further details on the simulation experiments.

C.49.1
Tree Generation Algorithms

The following two algorithms,
Algorithm

C.1
(many leaf nodes) and
Algorithm

C.2
(many branch nodes), details how the Type 1 and Type 2 trees are generated, respectively.

Algorithm C.1

Generating type 1 trees

procedure

Type1
(
p
)

A:=0\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}

for

j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}

do

for

i\in\{j+1,\ldots,p\}

do

if

\sum_{k=1}^{p}A_{ki}=0

then

if

i=j+1

then

A_{ji}:=1

else

A_{ji}:=\mathrm{Binomial}(\mathrm{success}=0.1)

end

if

else

A_{ji}:=0

end

if

end

for

end

for

return

A

end

procedure

Algorithm C.2

Generating type 2 trees

procedure

Type2
(
p
)

for

i\in\{2,\ldots,p\}

do

j:=\mathrm{sample}(\{1,\ldots,i-1\})

A_{ji}:=1

end

for

return

A

end

procedure

4.23.1.2

4.23.3
.

Section

4.23.3
.

C.50
Proofs

This section contains the proofs of all results presented in the main text.

C.50.1
Proofs of Section
4.19

Proof of
Lemma

4.1
:

Let
\theta=(\mathcal{G},(f_{i}),P_{N})\in\mathcal{T}_{p}\times\mathcal{D}_{3}^{p}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{G}}^{p}
. Furthermore, let all causal functions
(f_{i})
be nowhere constant and nonlinear. The additive noise is Gaussian, so the log density of
N_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,...,p\}
is given by

\displaystyle\nu_{i}(x)=-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma_{i}^{2})-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma_{i}^{2}},\quad\nu_{i}^{\prime}(x)=-\frac{x}{\sigma_{i}^{2}},\quad\nu_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x)=-\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}^{2}},\quad\nu_{i}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)=0.

By assumption we have that condition (i) of
Definition

4.2
is satisfied, hence assume for contradiction that condition (ii) of
Definition

4.2
is not satisfied. That is, we assume that there exists an
i\in\{1,...,p\}\setminus\{\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})\}
such that for all

\displaystyle(x,y)\in\mathcal{J}:=

\displaystyle\,\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\nu_{i}^{\prime\prime}(y-f_{i}(x))f^{\prime}_{i}(x)\not=0\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:f_{i}^{\prime}(x)\not=0\},

it holds that

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)-\xi^{\prime\prime}(x)\frac{f_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f_{i}^{\prime}(x)}-\frac{2f_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x)f_{i}^{\prime}(x)}{\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{y-f_{i}(x)}{\sigma^{2}}\left(f_{i}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)-\frac{(f_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x))^{2}}{f_{i}^{\prime}(x)}\right).

(C.1)

Henceforth, suppress the subscript
i
of
f_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}
.
First note that
\{x\in\mathbb{R}:f^{\prime}(x)=0\}
is closed by continuity of
f^{\prime}
. The complement is open, hence there exists a countable collection of mutually disjoint open intervals
(O_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\{x\in\mathbb{R}:f^{\prime}(x)\not=0\}=\cup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}O_{k}
. Since
f
is nowhere constant we know that
\{x\in\mathbb{R}:f^{\prime}(x)=0\}
has empty interior which implies that
\overline{\cup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}O_{k}}=\mathbb{R}
. Now let
(O_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
be indexed by
\mathbb{Z}
such that for any
k,j\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k<j
and
x\in O_{k},y\in O_{j}
it holds that
x<y
.
As the left-hand side of
Equation

C.1
is constant in
y
it must hold that

\displaystyle 0=f^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)-\frac{(f^{\prime\prime}(x))^{2}}{f^{\prime}(x)}=\frac{\frac{\partial f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{\partial x}f^{\prime}(x)-f^{\prime\prime}(x)\frac{\partial f^{\prime}(x)}{\partial x}}{(f^{\prime}(x))^{2}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}\right),

i.e.,
f^{\prime\prime}(x)/f^{\prime}(x)
is constant, for all
x\in\cup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}O_{k}
. On each
O_{k}
we have that

\displaystyle\partial/\partial x\log(\text{sign}(f^{\prime}(x))f^{\prime}(x))=c_{k,1}

\displaystyle\iff\log(\text{sign}(f^{\prime}(x))f^{\prime}(x))=c_{k,1}x+c_{k,2}

\displaystyle\iff\text{sign}(f^{\prime}(x))f^{\prime}(x)=\exp(c_{k,1}x+c_{k,2})

\displaystyle\iff f^{\prime}(x)=\pm\exp(c_{k,1}x+c_{k,2}).

Recall that we have assumed continuous differentiability of
f^{\prime}
. That is, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
t_{k}:=\sup(O_{k})=\inf(O_{k+1})
we have
\lim_{x\uparrow t_{k}}f^{\prime}(x)=\lim_{x\downarrow t_{k}}f^{\prime}(x)
and
\lim_{x\uparrow t_{k}}f^{\prime\prime}(x)=\lim_{x\downarrow t_{k}}f^{\prime\prime}(x)
. Assume without loss of generality that
f^{\prime}(x)=\exp(c_{k,1}x+c_{k,2})
for all
x\in O_{k}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. These conditions impose the restrictions
(c_{k,1}-c_{{k+1},1})t_{k}=c_{{k+1},2}-c_{k,2}
and
\log(c_{k,1}/c_{k+1,1})+(c_{k,1}-c_{k+1,1})t_{k}=c_{k+1,2}-c_{k,2}

which entails that
c_{k,1}=c_{k+1,1}
and
c_{k,2}=c_{k+1,2}
. This proves that there exists
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f^{\prime}(x)=\exp(c_{1}x+c_{2})
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus, the differential equation holds for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle 0=\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)-\xi^{\prime\prime}(x)\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}-\frac{2f^{\prime\prime}(x)f^{\prime}(x)}{\sigma^{2}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}\right)-2\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{\sigma^{2}},

by division with
f^{\prime}(x)
. By integration this implies that
0=\xi^{\prime\prime}(x)/f^{\prime}(x)-2f^{\prime}(x)/\sigma^{2}+c_{3}
such that
\xi^{\prime\prime}(x)=2\exp(2c_{1}x+2c_{2})/\sigma^{2}-c_{3}\exp(c_{1}x+c_{2})
and
\xi^{\prime}(x)=\exp(2c_{1}x+2c_{2})/c_{1}\sigma^{2}-c_{3}\exp(c_{1}x+c_{2})/c_{1}+c_{4}
and

\displaystyle\xi(x)=\frac{\exp(2c_{1}x+2c_{2})}{2c_{1}^{2}\sigma^{2}}-\frac{c_{3}\exp(c_{1}x+c_{2})}{c_{1}^{2}}+c_{4}x+c_{5}.

We see that
\xi(x)\to\infty\iff p_{X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\mathcal{G}}({i})}}}(x)\to\infty
as
x\to\text{sign}(c_{1})\cdot\infty
, in contradiction with the assumption that
p_{X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\mathcal{G}}({i})}}}(x)
is a probability density function if
c_{1}\not=0
. Thus, it must hold that
f^{\prime\prime}(x)/f^{\prime}(x)=0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, or equivalently, that
f
is a linear function, yielding a contradiction.

This proves that whenever
f_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{3}
is a nowhere constant and nonlinear function and the additive noise is Gaussian then condition (ii) of
Definition

4.2
is satisfied, so
\theta\in\Theta_{R}
.
\square

Proof of
Proposition

4.1
:

First, we consider the bivariate setting. Let
(X,Y)
be generated by an additive noise SCM
\theta\in\Theta_{R}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{2}\times\mathcal{D}_{3}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{3}}^{2}
given by
X:=N_{X}
and
Y:=f(X)+N_{Y}

with
P_{X}=p_{X}\cdot\lambda
and
P_{N_{Y}}=p_{N_{Y}}\cdot\lambda
having three times differentiable strictly positive densities and
f
is a three times differentiable nowhere constant function such that condition (ii) of
Definition

4.2
holds.

Assume for contradiction that we do not have observational identifiability of the causal structure
\mathcal{G}=(V=\{X,Y\},\mathcal{E}=\{(X\to Y)\})
. That is, there exists
\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{T}_{2}\times\mathcal{D}_{1}^{p}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}^{p}
with causal graph
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}\not=\mathcal{G}
or, equivalently, a differentiable function
g
and noise distributions
P_{\tilde{N}_{X}}=p_{\tilde{N}_{X}}\cdot\lambda
and
P_{\tilde{N}_{Y}}=p_{\tilde{N}_{Y}}\cdot\lambda
with continuous densities such that the structural assignments
\tilde{Y}:=\tilde{N}_{Y}
and
\tilde{X}:=g(\tilde{Y})+\tilde{N}_{X}
induce the same distribution, i.e.,

\displaystyle P_{X,Y}=P_{\tilde{X},\tilde{Y}}.

(C.2)

By the additive noise structural assignments we know that both
P_{X,Y}
and
P_{\tilde{X},\tilde{Y}}
have densities with respect to
\lambda^{2}
given by

\displaystyle p_{X,Y}(x,y)

\displaystyle=p_{X}(x)p_{N_{Y}}(y-f(x)),

\displaystyle p_{\tilde{X},\tilde{Y}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=p_{\tilde{N}_{X}}(x-g(y))p_{\tilde{Y}}(y),

for all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By the equality of distributions in
Equation

C.2
and strict positivity of
p_{X}
and
p_{N_{Y}}
we especially have that for
\lambda^{2}
-almost all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle 0<p_{X,Y}(x,y)=p_{\tilde{X},\tilde{Y}}(x,y).

(C.3)

However, as both
p_{X,Y}
and
p_{\tilde{X},\tilde{Y}}
are continuous we realize that the inequality in
Equation

C.3
holds for all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}
(if they were not everywhere equal there would exists a non-empty open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
on which they differ in contradiction with
\lambda^{2}
-almost everywhere equality).
Furthermore, by the assumption that
f
is three times differentiable and
p_{X}
,
p_{N_{Y}}
are three times continuously differentiable we have that
\partial^{3}\pi/\partial x^{3}
and
\partial^{3}\pi/\partial x^{2}\partial y
are well-defined partial-derivatives of

\displaystyle\pi(x,y):=\log p_{X,Y}(x,y)=\log p_{X}(x)+\log p_{N_{Y}}(y-f(x))=:\xi(x)+\nu(y-f(x)),

With
\tilde{\pi}(x,y):=\log p_{\tilde{X},\tilde{Y}}
we have that

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}(x,y)=\log p_{\tilde{N}_{X}}(x-g(y))+\log p_{\tilde{Y}}(y)=:\tilde{\xi}(x-g(y))+\tilde{\nu}(y).

Since it holds that
\pi=\tilde{\pi}
by
Equation

C.3
the partial-derivatives

\partial^{3}\tilde{\pi}/\partial x^{3}
and
\partial^{3}\tilde{\pi}/\partial x^{2}\partial y
are also well-defined. Now note that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle 0=\lim_{h\to 0}|\tilde{\pi}(x+h,y)-\tilde{\pi}(x,y)|/h=\lim_{h\to 0}|\tilde{\xi}(x-g(y)+h)-\tilde{\xi}(x-g(y))|/h,

implying that
\tilde{\xi}
is differentiable in
x-g(y)
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
or, equivalently,
\tilde{\xi}
is everywhere differentiable. Similar arguments yield that
\tilde{\xi}
is at least three times differentiable. We conclude that
\partial^{2}\tilde{\pi}(x,y)/\partial x^{2}=\tilde{\xi}^{\prime\prime}(x-g(y))
and
\partial^{2}\tilde{\pi}(x,y)/\partial x\partial y=-\tilde{\xi}^{\prime\prime}(x-g(y))g^{\prime}(y)
and for any
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\partial^{2}\tilde{\pi}(x,y)/\partial x\partial y\not=0
or, equivalently,

\forall(x,y)\in\mathcal{J}:=\left\{(x,y):\frac{\partial^{2}\pi(x,y)}{\partial x\partial y}=-\nu^{\prime\prime}(y-f(x))f^{\prime}(x)\not=0\right\},

it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\tilde{\pi}(x,y)}{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x\partial y}\tilde{\pi}(x,y)}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{-1}{g^{\prime}(y)}\right)=0.

It is worth noting that
\mathcal{J}\not=\emptyset
to ensure that the following derivations are not void of meaning. (This can be seen by noting that
f
is nowhere constant, i.e.,
f^{\prime}(x)\not=0
for
\lambda
-almost all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence,
\mathcal{J}=\emptyset
if and only if
p_{N_{Y}}
is a density such that
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:f^{\prime}(x)\not=0\}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\nu^{\prime\prime}(y-f(x))
is constantly zero or, equivalently,
\mathbb{R}\ni y\mapsto\nu^{\prime\prime}(y)
is constantly zero. This holds if and only if
p_{N_{Y}}
is either exponentially decreasing or exponentially increasing everywhere, which is a contradiction as no continuously differentiable function integrating to one has this property.) For any
(x,y)\in\mathcal{J}
we also have that

\displaystyle 0=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\pi(x,y)}{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x\partial y}\pi(x,y)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime}(x)+\nu^{\prime\prime}(y-f(x))f^{\prime}(x)^{2}-\nu^{\prime}(y-f(x))f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{-\nu^{\prime\prime}(y-f(x))f^{\prime}(x)}\right)

\displaystyle=-2f^{\prime\prime}+\frac{\nu^{\prime}f^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\nu^{\prime\prime}f^{\prime}}-\frac{\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\nu^{\prime\prime}f^{\prime}}+\frac{\nu^{\prime\prime\prime}\nu^{\prime}f^{\prime\prime}}{(\nu^{\prime\prime})^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\nu^{\prime\prime\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}}{(\nu^{\prime\prime})^{2}}-\frac{(f^{\prime\prime})^{2}\nu^{\prime}}{\nu^{\prime\prime}(f^{\prime})^{2}}+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}}{\nu^{\prime\prime}(f^{\prime})^{2}},

which implies that

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}=\xi^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime}}-\frac{f^{\prime}\nu^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\nu^{\prime\prime}}\right)-2\nu^{\prime\prime}f^{\prime\prime}f^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime}f^{\prime\prime\prime}+\frac{\nu^{\prime\prime\prime}\nu^{\prime}f^{\prime\prime}f^{\prime}}{\nu^{\prime\prime}}-\frac{\nu^{\prime}(f^{\prime\prime})^{2}}{f^{\prime}},

(C.4)

in contradiction with the assumption that condition (ii) of
Definition

4.2
holds. We conclude that
P_{X,Y}\not=P_{\tilde{X},\tilde{Y}}
.

Now consider a multivariate restricted causal model
\theta\in\Theta_{R}
over
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}
) with causal directed tree graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,\mathcal{E})
. Assume for contradiction that there exists an alternative SCM
\tilde{\theta}=(\tilde{\mathcal{G}},(\tilde{f}_{i}),P_{\tilde{N}})\in\mathcal{T}_{p}\times\mathcal{D}_{1}^{p}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}^{p}
inducing
\tilde{X}=(\tilde{X}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{X}_{p})
with causal graph
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}=(V,\tilde{\mathcal{E}})\not=\mathcal{G}
, such that
P_{X}=P_{\tilde{X}}
.

Any SCM induced distribution is Markov with respect to the underlying causal graph. As such, we have that
P_{X}
is Markov with respect to both
\mathcal{G}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
. Furthermore, since (in
\theta
) the causal functions are non-constant and the noise innovations have strictly positive density, we have, by Proposition 17 of
Peters et al. 2014
, that
P_{X}
satisfies causal minimality with respect to causal graph
\mathcal{G}
of
\theta
, i.e., it is globally Markov with respect to
\mathcal{G}
but not any proper subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
. If
P_{X}
also satisfies causal minimality with respect to
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
, then, by Proposition 29 of
Peters et al. 2014
, there exist
i,j\in V
such that
(j\to i)\in\mathcal{E}
and
(i\to j)\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
.

Assume for contradiction that
P_{X}
does not satisfy causal minimality with respect to
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
. By Proposition 4 of
Peters et al. 2014
, we have that there exists
(j^{\prime}\to i^{\prime})\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
such that
X_{j^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{i^{\prime}}
. Define
A:={\mathrm{nd}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({j^{\prime}})}\cup\{j^{\prime}\}
and
B:={\mathrm{de}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i^{\prime}})}\cup\{i^{\prime}\}
. It holds that
A{\perp\!\!\!\!\perp_{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}}(B\setminus\{i^{\prime}\})\,|\,i^{\prime}
, i.e.,
A
and
B\setminus\{i^{\prime}\}
are d-separated by
i^{\prime}
in the directed tree
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
. Since
P_{X}
is Markov with respect
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
it holds that
X_{A}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{B\setminus\{i^{\prime}\}}\,|\,X_{i^{\prime}}
, hence
X_{A}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{B}\,|\,X_{i^{\prime}}
.
Similarly, it holds that
X_{A}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{B}\,|\,X_{j^{\prime}}
which implies that
X_{A}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{i^{\prime}}\,|\,X_{j^{\prime}}
.
By applying the contraction property of conditional independence, we get that

\displaystyle X_{A}\,\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{i^{\prime}}\,|\,X_{j^{\prime}}\quad\text{and}\quad X_{i^{\prime}}\,\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{j^{\prime}}\implies X_{A}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{i^{\prime}},\text{ and }

\displaystyle X_{A}\,\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{B}\,|\,X_{i^{\prime}}\quad\text{and}\quad X_{A}\,\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{i^{\prime}}\implies X_{A}\,\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{B}.

Since
A\cup B=V,A\cap B=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{G}
is a directed tree (that spans
V
) there exist either an edge
(j^{\prime\prime}\to i^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{E}
with
j^{\prime\prime}\in A
and
i^{\prime\prime}\in B
or
j^{\prime\prime}\in B
and
i^{\prime\prime}\in A
. In either case, we have that
X_{i^{\prime\prime}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{j^{\prime\prime}}
, which contradicts
P_{X}
satisfying causal minimality with respect to
\mathcal{G}
. We conclude that
P_{X}
also satisfies causal minimality with respect to the alternative graph
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
.

Hence, the following structural equations hold for
(X_{i},X_{j})
and
(\tilde{X}_{i},\tilde{X}_{j})

\displaystyle X_{i}

\displaystyle=f_{i}(X_{j})+N_{i},\quad\text{with}\quad X_{j}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}N_{i},

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{j}

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}_{j}(\tilde{X}_{i})+\tilde{N}_{j},\quad\text{with}\quad\tilde{X}_{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\tilde{N}_{j},

with
P_{X_{j},X_{i}}=P_{\tilde{X}_{j},\tilde{X}_{i}}
. We can apply the same arguments as in the bivariate setup if we can argue that a density of
X_{j}
is three times differentiable and that a density of
\tilde{X}_{i}
is a continuous density.

To this end, note that the density
p_{X_{j}}
is given by the convolution of two densities

\displaystyle p_{X_{j}}(y)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}p_{f_{j}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\mathcal{G}}({j})}})}(t)p_{N_{j}}(y-t)\,dt,

(C.5)

as
X_{j}:=f_{j}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\mathcal{G}}({j})}})+N_{j}
with
X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\mathcal{G}}({j})}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}N_{j}
. Here we used that
f_{j}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\mathcal{G}}({j})}})
has density with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

To realize this note that
f_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{3}
and it is nowhere constant. By arguments similar to those in the proof of
Lemma

4.1
, this implies that
f^{\prime}(x)=0
at only countably many points
(d_{k})
. Now let
(O_{k})
be the countable collection of mutually disjoint open intervals that cover
\mathbb{R}
except for the points
(d_{k})
. By continuity of
f^{\prime}
we know that
f^{\prime}(x)
is either strictly positive or strictly negative on each
O_{k}
. That is,
f
is continuously differentiable and strictly monotone on each
O_{k}
. Thus,
f
has a continuously differentiable inverse on each
O_{k}
by, e.g., the inverse function theorem. This ensures that
f_{j}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\mathcal{G}}({j})}})
has a density with respect to the Lebesgue measure whenever
X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\mathcal{G}}({j})}}
does. By starting at the root node
X_{\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})}=N_{\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})}
, which by assumption has a density, we can iteratively apply the above argumentation down the directed path from
\mathrm{rt}(\mathcal{G})
to
j
in order to conclude that any
X_{j}
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
has a density with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

Since
p_{N_{j}}
is assumed strictly positive three times continuous differentiable, the representation in
Equation

C.5
furthermore yields that
p_{X_{j}}
is three times differentiable; see, e.g., Theorem 11.4 and 11.5 of
Schilling 2017
.

Now we argue that
\tilde{X}_{i}
has a continuous density. First note that
P_{X_{i}}
at least has a continuous density
p_{X_{i}}
by arguments similar to those applied for
Equation

C.5
. By the assumption that
P_{X}=P_{\tilde{X}}
we especially have that
P_{X_{i}}=P_{\tilde{X}_{i}}
which implies that also
\tilde{X}_{i}
has a continuous density. By virtue of the arguments for the bivariate setup we arrive at a contradiction, so it must hold that
P_{X}\not=P_{\tilde{X}}
.

\square

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
:

Consider an SCM
\tilde{\theta}=(\tilde{\mathcal{G}},(\tilde{f}_{i}),P_{\tilde{N}})\in\{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}\}\times\mathcal{D}_{1}^{p}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{G}}^{p}
with
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}\not=\mathcal{G}
and let
Q_{\tilde{\theta}}
be the induced distribution.
As
Q_{\tilde{\theta}}
is Markov with respect to
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
and generated by an additive noise model the density
q_{\tilde{\theta}}
factorizes as

\displaystyle q_{\tilde{\theta}}(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{p}q_{\tilde{\theta}}(x_{i}|x_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})=\prod_{i=1}^{p}q_{\tilde{N}_{i}}(x_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(x_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})).

The cross entropy between
P_{X}
and
Q_{\tilde{\theta}}
is then given by

\displaystyle h(P_{X},Q_{\tilde{\theta}})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[-\log\left(q_{\tilde{\theta}}(X)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[-\log\left(q_{\tilde{N}_{i}}\left(X_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{p}h\left(X_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}}),\tilde{N}_{i}\right),

where the latter is a sum of the cross entropies between the distribution of
X_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})
and the distribution of
\tilde{N}_{i}
.
As
Q_{\tilde{\theta}}
is generated by a Gaussian noise structural causal model, we have for all
1\leq i\leq p
that
\tilde{N}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2})
for some
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}>0
. Hence for all
1\leq i\leq p
,

\displaystyle h\left(X_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}}),\tilde{N}_{i}\right)=

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}\left[-\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma_{i}}\exp\left(-\frac{\left(X_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})\right)^{2}}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\log(\sqrt{2\pi}\tilde{\sigma}_{i})+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})\right)^{2}\right]}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}}.

Thus, for given set of causal functions
(\tilde{f}_{i})
and a fixed
i
, the noise variance that minimizes the cross entropy is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{i}=\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})\right)^{2}\right]}.

We thus have

\displaystyle\inf_{\tilde{\sigma}_{i}>0}\left\{\log(\sqrt{2\pi}\tilde{\sigma}_{i})+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})\right)^{2}\right]}{2\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\,\log\left(\sqrt{2\pi}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})\right)^{2}\right]\right)+\frac{1}{2}.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\,\inf_{Q\in\{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}\}\times\mathcal{D}_{1}^{p}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{G}}^{p}}h(P_{X},Q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,p\log(\sqrt{2\pi})+\frac{p}{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\inf_{\tilde{f}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})\right)^{2}\right]\right).

Finally, as
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is dense in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(P_{X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}}})
, we have that

\displaystyle\inf_{\tilde{f}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{i}-\mathbb{E}[X_{i}|X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}}])\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\inf_{\tilde{f}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{E}[X_{i}|X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}}]-\tilde{f}_{i}(X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{i}-\mathbb{E}[X_{i}|X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}}])\right)^{2}\right].

Here we used that
X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}}
has density with respect to the Lebesgue measure,
P_{X_{{\mathrm{pa}^{\tilde{\mathcal{G}}}({i})}}}\ll\lambda
